Exam Relevance
RBI Grade B — Highest-priority current-affairs item. Expect direct questions on the August 2026 numbers
and descriptive questions on monetary policy transmission, the neutral stance and managing external
shocks. Master the statutory scaffolding: Section 45ZB (constitution), 45ZA (target), 45ZN (failure report),
and the corridor mechanics. The strongest analytical themes are (i) why monetary policy is a blunt
instrument against supply shocks; (ii) the rupee–capital flows–US yields channel; and (iii) the "neither
dovish nor hawkish" framing as an articulation of what a neutral stance means in practice.
3. RBI to Resume Licensing of Urban Cooperative Banks
Source: The Hindu
Context
Announced alongside the August 2026 monetary policy, the RBI will resume licensing of Urban
Cooperative Banks (UCBs) on an "on tap" basis after a pause of more than two decades, as part of
developing a vibrant cooperative sector. Governor Sanjay Malhotra said draft guidelines would be
issued shortly for stakeholder consultation.
What It Is
Three developmental and regulatory measures announced alongside the monetary policy:
1. On-tap licensing of UCBs
A discussion paper on Licensing of UCBs was published on 13 January 2026 for stakeholder feedback;
the decision follows that feedback.
There are 1,457 urban cooperative banks as per RBI and NABARD.
Fresh UCB licensing had been paused since 2004, following governance failures and bank collapses in
the sector.
"On tap" means the application window stays continuously open — applicants may apply at any time
rather than only in specific rounds. The RBI already follows this for universal banks and small finance
banks.
Indicative eligibility criteria proposed in the draft for credit cooperative societies seeking conversion:
Criterion Threshold
Minimum deposit base ₹10,000 crore
Minimum net worth ₹300 crore
Years in existence At least 10 years
CRAR At least 12%
Net NPA ratio Not more than 3%
Registration Multi-State Co-operative Societies Act, 2002
Multi-state societies will be considered first; select uni-state societies with a wider footprint may follow.
No member may hold more than 5% of share capital; directors may not hold executive positions.
Applications will be made through the RBI's PRAVAAH portal; in-principle approval is valid for 18 months.
2. Review of concentration risk norms for RCBs
Currently governed by the Credit Monitoring Arrangement (CMA) instructions of 2008.
The banking sector generally, and cooperative banking in particular, have expanded and changed
significantly over 18 years, requiring a fresh look at prudential norms.
Objective: develop a vibrant cooperative sector while addressing prudential concerns arising from
concentrated lending.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 44 of 200
“Read the news like an examiner: who, what, when, how much.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Draft amendment directions to be issued for wider consultation.
Background: Cooperative Banking Structure
Tier Institutions
Urban Cooperative Banks Single-state and multi-state; urban and semi-urban areas
Rural Cooperative Banks (short-term) State Cooperative Banks → District Central Cooperative Banks → Primary
Agricultural Credit Societies
Rural Cooperative Banks (long-term) State and Primary Cooperative Agriculture and Rural Development Banks
Dual regulation: the RBI regulates banking functions under the Banking Regulation Act, 1949;
incorporation, management and audit rest with the Registrar of Cooperative Societies (State) or the
Central Registrar (multi-state).
The Banking Regulation (Amendment) Act, 2020 expanded RBI powers over cooperative banks after
the PMC Bank crisis.
DICGC deposit insurance of ₹5 lakh per depositor per bank covers cooperative banks.
NABARD supervises State Cooperative Banks and District Central Cooperative Banks.
Exam Relevance
NABARD Grade A — Very high relevance. NABARD supervises State Cooperative Banks and District
Central Cooperative Banks, and the review of concentration risk norms for RCBs falls squarely in its
domain. Know the CMA instructions of 2008, the rural cooperative structure, PACS computerisation, and
NABARD's refinance role. Expect direct questions on the UCB count (1,457) and on the RCB concentration-
risk review.
RBI Grade B — Highest-priority item alongside the policy decision itself.
Agriculture
1. Framework for Fertilizer Sale (FFS)
Source: Business Standard
Context
A new digital platform for transparent and farmer-centric fertiliser distribution has been implemented
on a pilot basis in 56 districts across 23 states, Minister of State for Chemicals and Fertilisers Anupriya
Patel informed the Rajya Sabha on 4 August 2026 in a written reply. The pilot has expanded rapidly — it
covered 40 districts across 20 states as of late July 2026.
What It Is
The "Framework for Fertilizer Sale" (FFS) is jointly developed by the Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilisers
(Department of Fertilizers) and the Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare, with the consent of
state governments, and rolled out in a staggered pilot manner.
Objective to make fertiliser distribution more transparent, organised, farmer-centric, land- and crop-
data integrated, and demand-driven.
How It Works
Registration — the farmer or an authorised representative registers through a mobile application,1.
entering land and crop details.
Pre-booking — advance booking of fertiliser requirements against those details.2.
Token generation — a QR code-based token is generated on successful booking.3.
Retail verification — the retailer scans the QR code through a Point of Sale (PoS) machine.4.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 45 of 200
“Read the news like an examiner: who, what, when, how much.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Distribution — fertiliser is released after Aadhaar-based biometric authentication, per existing5.
procedure.
Supporting Digital Infrastructure
Integrated Fertilizer Management System (iFMS) — an online platform for real-time monitoring and
management of fertiliser movement across the country, tracking dispatches, allocation, rake
placement, stock position and state-wise supply status by states and stakeholders. FFS purchase
reports feed into iFMS dashboards.
AgriStack / Farmer Registry — a registry of about 102.8 million (10.28 crore) farmer IDs is being
integrated with PM-KISAN, the Jan Samarth platform for Kisan Credit Card loans, and MSP-based
crop procurement systems.
Exam Relevance
NABARD Grade A — Very high relevance for Phase II Paper II. Know the DBT mechanics, the 2025-26
figures (746.57 LMT, 8.4 crore farmers, 2.87 lakh retailers, ₹2.17 lakh crore subsidy), the 50-bag limit and
state variations, and AgriStack's three registries (farmer registry, geo-referenced village maps, crop-
sown registry). The Jan Samarth–KCC linkage is directly relevant to NABARD's credit mandate, as is the
exclusion risk for tenant farmers — connect to Joint Liability Groups as the standard workaround for
landless cultivators.

Context
NITI Aayog released a report titled "Reimagining Care: Strategies for Empowering Caregivers in Viksit
Bharat@2047."
Who Are Caregivers
Per the WHO definition adopted in the report, caregivers are individuals who provide physical,
emotional and social support to people who cannot independently perform activities of daily living due
to age, chronic illness or disability.
Type Description
Formal caregivers Trained professionals — personal care aides, home health aides, geriatric assistants —
delivering non-medical or clinical support in homes, hospitals or institutions for
remuneration
Informal caregivers Family members, friends or community volunteers providing unpaid, non-formal long-term
care
The Indian Landscape
Indicator Figure
Elderly population (60+) 149 million (10.5%) in 2022 → projected 347 million (20.8%) by 2050
Persons with disabilities Over 26.8 million (2.21%), with 21 benchmark disability categories under the
RPwD Act, 2016
Unpaid care time (Time Use Survey) Women 299 minutes/day against men's 97 minutes/day
Care services market USD 29.62 billion (2023) → USD 72.31 billion by 2030, at a CAGR of 13.76%
The demographic point: India's elderly share doubles from about 10% to 21% within a single generation.
Countries that aged at this pace — Japan, China — did so at far higher per capita income. India will face
a large long-term care burden before it becomes rich enough to fund it institutionally.
Types of Caregivers in India
Family-based informal caregivers — relatives providing unpaid assistance within households;
traditionally the backbone of India's social fabric.
Community-based informal caregivers — local volunteers, extended kin and Self-Help Groups, such
as Kudumbashree's K4 Care and Kerala's Neighbourhood Network in Palliative Care.
Institutional formal caregivers — trained staff in old-age homes, rehabilitation centres and hospital
geriatric wards.
Home-based and specialised palliative caregivers — paid professionals providing bedside
assistance, dementia support and chronic illness management at the patient's residence.
2. Vehicle-to-Vehicle (V2V) Technology
Source: Business Standard
Context The Ministry of Road Transport and Highways (MoRTH) issued a draft notification proposing
amendments to the Central Motor Vehicles Rules, 1989, to introduce Vehicle-to-Vehicle communication

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 51 of 200
“The morning hour is worth three at night.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
technology in Indian vehicles.
What It Is
An advanced wireless communication framework enabling nearby motor vehicles to continuously
broadcast and receive real-time data on speed, location, direction, acceleration and braking status.
Unlike traditional sensors — cameras and radar — which require a direct line of sight, V2V enables
proactive accident prevention by warning drivers about hazards beyond their visual range.
Aim: to reduce road fatalities and accidents by complementing existing Advanced Driver Assistance
Systems (ADAS), expanding situational awareness, and building a foundation for broader Intelligent
Transportation Systems (ITS).
How It Works
On-Board Units (OBUs): factory-fitted units continuously transmit low-latency data packets
containing GPS coordinates, velocity, trajectory and status alerts.
Peer-to-peer data mesh: transmissions occur directly between nearby vehicles within a range of
several hundred metres, without relying on cellular towers or cloud servers.
Real-time risk computation: an onboard processor analyses received data against the host vehicle's
own vector, calculating collision risk within milliseconds and triggering visual, audio or haptic
warnings before the hazard is visible.
3. The Clouded Leopard
Source: News on Air
Context On International Clouded Leopard Day (observed 4 August), the Ministry of Environment, Forest
and Climate Change launched the Clouded Leopard Conservation Action Plan (CAP).
About the Conservation Action Plan
A comprehensive, multi-state strategic framework to secure habitats, restore ecological corridors,
standardise population assessments, and empower local communities for the species' long-term
survival in India.
Aim: to halt population decline, protect critical forest landscapes across Northeast India, establish
reliable population baselines through advanced science, and mitigate human-wildlife conflict
outside formal protected areas.
About the Species
The Clouded Leopard (Neofelis nebulosa) is an evolutionarily ancient, medium-sized wild cat bridging
the gap between big cats (Panthera) and small cats (Felinae). Molecular research suggests it branched
from the Pantherinae lineage over 6 million years ago, making it one of the most ancient living feline
species.
Distribution and habitat
Found from Nepal, Bhutan, Bangladesh and Northeast India through Southeast Asia (Indochina,
Sumatra, Borneo) to southern China.
Prefers lowland dense tropical rainforest, but adapts to dry woodlands, mangrove swamps (in
Borneo), and Himalayan foothill forests up to 2,743 metres.
Conservation and legal status
Framework Status
IUCN Red List Vulnerable
Wild Life (Protection) Act, 1972 Schedule I

5. GOBARdhan — National Circular Bioenergy Scheme
Source: PIB
Context The Union Cabinet approved the National Circular Bioenergy Scheme – GOBARdhan, with an
outlay of ₹23,731 crore, to catalyse the growth of compressed biogas (CBG).
What It Is
GOBARdhan — Galvanising Organic Bio-Agro Resources Dhan — a scheme to raise domestic CBG
production almost tenfold through assured offtake, administered pricing, capital subsidies, pipeline
infrastructure and easier access to finance.
Parameter Detail
Outlay ₹23,731 crore
Period FY 2026-27 to FY 2035-36
Administering Ministry Petroleum and Natural Gas
Feedstock Agricultural residue, cattle dung, sugar industry press mud, municipal organic waste and
other biomass
Output CBG, blended into CNG (transport) and PNG (domestic); plus organic manure
Origin: GOBARdhan was originally launched under the Swachh Bharat Mission (Grameen) in 2018 as a
solid and liquid waste management component. It has now been recast as a national clean-energy and
circular-economy programme.
Objective
Convert cattle dung, agricultural residues and other organic waste into Compressed Biogas (CBG)
and organic manure.
Increase domestic CBG production by nearly 10 times.
Reduce dependence on imported natural gas and strengthen energy security.
Promote waste-to-wealth and a circular bioeconomy.
Mobilise large-scale private investment and generate rural employment.

Banking and Finance
1. The Closing Auction Session (CAS)
Source: Times of India
Context The Closing Auction Session was introduced by Indian stock exchanges for cash market stocks
with derivatives, following SEBI's directive of 16 January 2026. It became effective from 3 August 2026,
launched simultaneously by the NSE and BSE.
What It Is
A dedicated 20-minute daily auction mechanism in the cash market segment for equity stocks on
which derivative (F&O) contracts are available. Instead of computing closing prices through
continuous trade averages, CAS aggregates buy and sell orders to discover a single, fair equilibrium
closing price.
Aim: to curb end-of-day price manipulation, improve price discovery, align Indian equity markets
with global best practices, and deliver more accurate benchmark values for mutual funds, ETFs and
derivatives settlement.
How It Works
Phase Timing What happens
Phase 1 — Transition
and Reference Price
3:15 – 3:20
PM
Continuous trading ends for eligible F&O stocks. A reference price is
computed from the VWAP of trades between 3:00 and 3:15 PM, with a
strict ±3% price band
Phase 2 — Order Entry 3:20 – 3:30
PM
3:20–3:25 PM: market and limit orders may be placed, modified or
cancelled. 3:25–3:30 PM: only limit orders accepted; market orders
cannot be altered or cancelled. The window closes at a random time
between 3:28 and 3:30 PM to prevent order spoofing
Phase 3 — Order
Matching and Price
Discovery
3:30 – 3:35
PM
Orders matched at a single equilibrium price maximising executable
volume; market orders get execution priority over limit orders
2. Licensing of Urban Cooperative Banks
Source: BS
Context
The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) is set to resume licensing of urban cooperative banks (UCBs), after a
pause of more than two decades, as part of developing a vibrant co-operative sector.
The Legal Basis
Licensing of UCBs is the regulatory authorisation granted by the RBI under Section 22 read with Section
56 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949. It empowers primary cooperative credit societies to conduct
banking — accepting public deposits and granting credit — in urban and semi-urban areas.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 55 of 200
“The morning hour is worth three at night.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Section 56 is the provision that applies the Banking Regulation Act to cooperative societies with
modifications.
Regulatory Timeline
Year Development
1966 UCBs brought under RBI's regulatory ambit via amendments to the BR Act
May 1993 – June 2001 Liberalised licensing era — over 820 new UCB licences issued
2004 RBI suspended new UCB licensing following widespread failures
January 2026 RBI released a comprehensive Discussion Paper on reopening licensing
August 2026 Governor announced draft guidelines for on-tap licensing based on stakeholder feedback
Why Licensing Was Halted in 2004
Widespread insolvency: nearly 31% of UCBs licensed during the 1990s liberalisation turned financially
unsound within a short period, owing to poor credit appraisal and rapid unviable expansion.
Governance failures and frauds: the Madhavpura Mercantile Cooperative Bank crisis (2001) —
linked to the Ketan Parekh stock scam — exposed severe corporate governance deficits, conflict of
interest and insider lending.
Dual regulation gaps: banking operations were supervised by the RBI while administrative and
managerial oversight rested with State Registrars of Cooperative Societies, creating enforcement
delays and supervisory loopholes.
Why a Restart Is Now Feasible
Strengthened statutory powers: the Banking Regulation (Amendment) Act, 2020 gave the RBI direct
powers over UCB management, board constitution and resolution — effectively closing the dual-
regulation loophole.
Four-tier prudential framework: deposit-based tiered regulatory standards (Tiers 1 to 4) in force
since December 2022, ensuring calibrated risk oversight.
Improved sector balance sheets: gross NPAs of UCBs fell to a six-year low of ₹21,769 crore in FY26,
with higher provisioning coverage ratios and capital buffers.
Institutional backing: the National Urban Cooperative Finance and Development Corporation
(NUCFDC), established in 2024, offers shared technology platforms, liquidity support and capital
mobilisation for UCBs.
Practice MCQs
Q1. With reference to the licensing of Urban Cooperative Banks, consider the following statements:
a. Licensing is granted under Section 22 read with Section 56 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949.
b. UCBs were brought under the RBI's regulatory ambit in 1966.
c. Over 820 new UCB licences were issued between 1993 and 2001.
d. The RBI suspended fresh UCB licensing in 2004.
How many of the above statements are correct? (a) Only one (b) Only two (c) Only three (d) All four (e)
None
Answer Key
(d) — All four are correct. Section 56 is the key provision — it applies the Banking Regulation Act to1.
cooperative societies with modifications.
Exam Relevance
RBI Grade B — Very high relevance. Master the four-tier framework (Tier 1: deposits up to ₹100 crore; Tier
2: ₹100–1,000 crore; Tier 3: ₹1,000–10,000 crore; Tier 4: above ₹10,000 crore), the 2020 Amendment's

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 56 of 200
“The morning hour is worth three at night.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
specific powers, NUCFDC's role, and the Madhavpura episode as the reference failure.
3. The Bankers' Books Evidence Bill, 2026
Source: The Hindu
Context The Lok Sabha passed the Bankers' Books Evidence Bill, 2026 by voice vote amid Opposition
protests. Introduced by the Finance Minister on 3 August 2026, it replaces the 135-year-old colonial-era
Bankers' Books Evidence Act, 1891, aligning legal evidence standards with modern digital, virtual and
cloud-based banking.
What It Is
A framework regulating how bank records are admitted as valid evidence in legal proceedings. It
allows certified physical and electronic extracts of bank records to be presented in court without
requiring original ledgers or summoning bank officials as routine witnesses.
Aim: to modernise India's banking evidence law, incorporate technology-neutral provisions for digital
records, speed up judicial proceedings in financial disputes, and reduce operational burdens on
banking personnel.
Key Features
Expanded definition of "bankers' books" — broadened beyond paper ledgers to cover all record
formats, including electronic, digital, virtual and cloud-based databases.
Admissibility and integrity conditions for electronic records — enforceable provided they:
are a true representation of the data,
show no unauthorised data changes, and
reflect no system tampering.
Standardised digital certification — electronic records submitted to courts must carry a technical
certificate detailing the generating computer system, authenticated by manual or digital signatures
of designated branch or office heads.
Protection against compulsory court attendance — bank officers cannot be compelled to produce
original books or appear as witnesses where the bank is not a party, unless ordered by a judge for a
strictly defined special cause.
Power to extend coverage — empowers the Central Government to extend the Act to other financial
sector institutions by notification.
3. RBI's Draft Framework on Credit Valuation Adjustment Risk
Source: Business Standard
Context The Reserve Bank of India has proposed a revised framework governing how commercial
banks must set aside capital against the risk of losses on their derivative trades, replacing rules in
place since 2011. Comments have been invited until 28 August, with the rules proposed to take effect
from 1 April 2027.
Applicability: all commercial banks, excluding small finance banks, payments banks and local area
banks.
What CVA Is
Credit Valuation Adjustment (CVA) is an adjustment banks make to the price of a derivative contract
to account for the possibility that the counterparty on the other side of the trade could default. The
capital charge ensures banks set aside enough capital to cover such potential losses.
In simple terms: if a bank enters a ten-year interest rate swap with a corporate, the swap may be worth
money to the bank — but only if the corporate is still solvent when payment falls due. CVA is the value the

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 57 of 200
“The morning hour is worth three at night.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
bank subtracts to reflect that risk, and the CVA capital charge is the buffer held against the risk that this
adjustment itself moves adversely as the counterparty's credit quality deteriorates.
Why the Change
The existing framework was based on Basel Committee on Banking Supervision (BCBS) standards
issued in 2010.
The BCBS has since revised its guidelines as part of the final Basel III framework, prompting the RBI to
update its rules to align with global standards.
The Proposed Approach
Banks must use the Basic Approach for CVA (BA-CVA), with a choice between two versions:
Version Feature Suited to
Reduced BA-CVA Does not recognise any hedges taken against CVA risk Banks that do not hedge
this risk
Full BA-CVA Allows banks to factor in eligible hedges — such as single-name
and index credit default swaps — provided they meet specific
conditions linking the hedge to the counterparty
Banks that actively hedge
CVA risk
The Simplified Treatment for Smaller Books
Banks whose aggregate notional amount of non-centrally cleared derivatives is ₹10 trillion or less
may skip the BA-CVA computation altogether.
Instead, they may set their CVA capital requirement equal to 100% of their capital requirement for
counterparty credit risk.
Such banks cannot recognise any CVA hedges under this simpler treatment.
The RBI's supervisory arm can deny a bank this option if it finds the bank's CVA risk material to its
overall risk profile.
Background Concepts
Derivative: a contract whose value derives from an underlying asset, rate or index — interest rate
swaps, currency forwards, credit default swaps.
Counterparty credit risk: the risk that the other party to a derivative defaults before final settlement.
Distinct from ordinary credit risk because the exposure varies with market movements and can be
positive or negative.
Central clearing: trades routed through a central counterparty (in India, the Clearing Corporation of
India Ltd) are far less risky, since the CCP guarantees performance. This is why the ₹10 trillion threshold
applies specifically to non-centrally cleared derivatives — those are where CVA risk actually resides.
Credit default swap (CDS): a contract that pays out if a specified entity defaults — effectively
insurance against counterparty failure, and hence an eligible CVA hedge.
Basel III: the post-2008 global regulatory framework covering capital adequacy, leverage and
liquidity, issued by the BCBS, which is hosted by the Bank for International Settlements at Basel.
Exam Relevance
RBI Grade B — Very high relevance for Phase II Paper III. This is core prudential regulation and precisely
the technical territory RBI papers reward.
4. RBI's NBFC Upper Layer List 2026-27
Source: The Hindu
Context The Reserve Bank of India released a list of 17 large (Upper Layer) non-banking finance
companies for 2026-27, which included Tata Sons — subjecting them to enhanced regulatory norms for

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 58 of 200
“The morning hour is worth three at night.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
at least five years and mandatory listing within three years of identification. The RBI added that Tata
Sons' status "is without prejudice to the outcome of its application for de-registration, which is under
examination."
Scale-Based Regulation
Scale-Based Regulation (SBR) is a regulatory approach in which financial institutions are regulated
according to their size, complexity, interconnectedness and risk profile, rather than applying identical
rules to every institution.
Under the RBI's Scale Based Regulation (SBR) framework, NBFCs are placed in four layers, with regulatory
intensity rising by size, activity and perceived risk.
Layer Composition
Base Layer (NBFC-BL) Smaller, non-deposit-taking NBFCs below the asset threshold
Middle Layer (NBFC-ML) Deposit-taking NBFCs, HFCs, IFCs, IDFs, SPDs and larger non-deposit-taking NBFCs
Upper Layer (NBFC-UL) NBFCs specifically identified annually by the RBI as warranting enhanced regulatory
requirements
Top Layer (NBFC-TL) Ordinarily empty; populated only if the RBI judges a substantial increase in systemic risk
from specific UL NBFCs
Consequences of UL classification
Enhanced regulatory norms for a minimum of five years, even if the NBFC later falls below the
parametric benchmarks.
Mandatory listing within three years of identification.
Additional requirements including a Common Equity Tier 1 ratio, large exposure norms, differential
provisioning, an independent compliance function, and internal capital adequacy assessment.

2. India's First T2T Genome of Pigeonpea
Source: businessline
Context The Indian Council of Agricultural Research announced that it has developed India's first fully
annotated telomere-to-telomere (T2T) reference genome of pigeonpea (Cajanus cajan L. Millsp.),
variety 'Asha'.
What It Is
A complete, gap-free genome sequence covering every chromosome from one telomere to the other,
including the centromeres — as distinct from a draft genome, which leaves gaps in repetitive regions
that older sequencing technology could not resolve.
Parameter Detail
Developed by ICAR–National Institute for Plant Biotechnology (NIPB), New Delhi
Size 752.65 million base pairs
Chromosomes covered All 11, including centromeres and telomeres
Assembly designation NIPB_CcT2T_4, deposited in the global database NCBI
Why a T2T Genome Is Different
A draft genome sequences the readable parts of the DNA and leaves gaps wherever the sequence
becomes repetitive — precisely where centromeres and telomeres sit. Short-read sequencing cannot
resolve long stretches of near-identical repeats.
A T2T assembly closes those gaps, reading each chromosome continuously end to end. The 92
contigs for 11 chromosomes indicates near-complete chromosome-level assembly, and the 99.9%
transcriptome read alignment is the accuracy check — nearly every RNA sequence maps cleanly
onto the assembly.
Why the gaps mattered: centromeric and repetitive regions govern chromosome segregation,
recombination frequency and the behaviour of large gene families — including many disease-
resistance genes, which cluster in repeat-rich regions. A breeder working from a draft was effectively
blind in exactly the areas where stress-tolerance and resistance traits often reside.
The Institutional Track Record
2011 — ICAR–NIPB published the world's first draft genome of pigeonpea variety 'Asha' — the first crop
genome sequenced entirely in India.
2017 — an improved draft genome was published.
Now — the fully annotated T2T assembly.
Why Pigeonpea Matters
Popularly known as Arhar or Tur, it is one of India's most important pulse crops and a major source of
dietary protein, central to food and nutritional security. India remains a net importer of pulses.
3. Proposed Central Law on Women Farmers' Rights
Source: The Hindu
Context The Union government is considering a separate legislation to protect the rights of women

farmers, on the lines of the Maharashtra Women Farmers Empowerment Act, 2026, said S. Mahendra

The Core Problem
For decades, the word "farmer" in government records meant the person who owns the land. Since
agricultural land is predominantly registered in the names of male family members, women who sow,
transplant, weed, harvest, rear livestock and handle post-harvest processing remained legally
invisible — and therefore ineligible for institutional credit, subsidies, crop insurance, extension services
and most farmer-centric schemes.
In Maharashtra, women hold only about 15.5% of operational farmland, though they perform a large
share of agricultural labour.
The Maharashtra Model
Feature Detail
Status First Indian State law dedicated to women farmers; passed unanimously by the
Legislative Assembly and later the Legislative Council
Introduced by Agriculture Minister Dattatraya Bharane
Core instrument Woman Farmer Certificate — formal recognition as a farmer irrespective of land
ownership
Benefits unlocked Government welfare schemes, agricultural subsidies, institutional credit, crop insurance,
extension services and financial assistance
Institutions created Women Farmers Database; Maharashtra State Fund for Women Farmers; Women
Farmers Empowerment Cell; Women Farmers Empowerment Council; State Monitoring
Committee
Local role Gram Panchayats and local institutions identify eligible women farmers and facilitate
certification
Definition of
agriculture
Broad — crop cultivation, seed production, animal husbandry, poultry, dairy, livestock
rearing, apiculture, fisheries, aquaculture, sericulture, vermiculture, horticulture, floriculture,
mushroom cultivation and agro-forestry
The Fund receives grants from the Central and State Governments, donations and other sources, and
involves recurring expenditure from the Consolidated Fund of the State.

General 5  ·  Agriculture 2  ·  Facts To Remember 15
Context In a world-first milestone, five captive-bred Slender-billed Vultures along with five White-
rumped Vultures were released into the wild using the "Soft Release" scientific method in the Biswanath
Wildlife Division of Kaziranga National Park, Assam.
Slender-billed Vulture (Gyps tenuirostris)
An Old World scavenger bird of prey of the family Accipitridae. Once considered a subspecies of the
Indian Vulture under the name Long-billed Vulture, it is now classified as a distinct species native to
South Asia and parts of Southeast Asia.
Aspect Detail
IUCN status Critically Endangered
Habitat Savannas, open arid landscapes, dry deciduous forests and river valleys, primarily at lower
elevations; avoids dense human habitation but visits slaughterhouses and garbage dumps for
carrion
Distribution Sub-Himalayan regions of northern and eastern India (notably Assam), Nepal, Bangladesh, Myanmar
and Cambodia
Key features: an unfeathered, narrow, elongated black head and long skinny black neck, giving a scruffy
appearance; a distinct shiny, slender beak adapted for deep carcass feeding; dark brown, light brown
and beige plumage with cream-coloured feathering on the legs visible in flight.
White-rumped Vulture (Gyps bengalensis)
The smallest of the Old World Gyps vultures, native to South and Southeast Asia. Formerly among the
most abundant large birds of prey in the world during the 1980s, its global population collapsed by over
99% within two decades due to veterinary drug toxicity.
Aspect Detail
IUCN status Critically Endangered
Habitat Highly adaptable — open landscapes, grasslands, open deciduous forests, agricultural areas;
uniquely adapted to live close to human settlements and slaughterhouse grounds
Distribution Northern and central India, Pakistan, Nepal, Bangladesh, and localised pockets of Southeast Asia
Key features:
Signature white patch on the lower back/rump and underwing coverts, visible in flight or when
stretching wings.
Overall dark/black plumage with a whitish downy ruff or collar at the base of the neck.
12 tail feathers, against 14 in most other Gyps species.
Lethal drug vulnerability — highly susceptible to renal failure from consuming livestock carcasses
treated with NSAIDs such as diclofenac, aceclofenac, ketoprofen and nimesulide.
The Vulture Crisis
The 99% collapse was traced to diclofenac, used to treat cattle. Vultures feeding on treated carcasses
suffered visceral gout and kidney failure. India banned veterinary diclofenac in 2006, and later

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 66 of 200
“The day you do not feel like studying is the day it counts most.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
ketoprofen and aceclofenac; meloxicam is the approved vulture-safe alternative.
Why it mattered beyond the birds: vultures removed millions of tonnes of carcass waste. Their
disappearance allowed feral dog and rat populations to expand, raising rabies incidence and
carcass-borne disease — one of the most cited examples of a collapsed ecosystem service with
direct human health and economic cost.
Conservation framework: the Action Plan for Vulture Conservation (2020–2025); Vulture
Conservation Breeding Centres (Pinjore in Haryana, Rajabhatkhawa in West Bengal, Rani in Assam
and others) run with the Bombay Natural History Society; Vulture Safe Zones; and Schedule I
protection under the Wild Life (Protection) Act, 1972.

Source: Times of India

What It Is

3. E-Samudra Platform
Source: PIB
Context The Union Minister for Ports, Shipping and Waterways launched E-Samudra, an integrated digital

Aim: to transition maritime governance from a paper-based office model to a transparent, predictable
digital ecosystem, enhancing Ease of Living for Indian seafarers and Ease of Doing Business for
maritime businesses.
Key Features
Single-window digital governance — consolidates administrative workflows, online fee payments,
real-time application tracking and digital certificate issuance into a single paperless portal.
Digital Seafarers Employment Agreement (d-SEA) — verifiable digital employment agreements
safeguarding contractual entitlements, transparent wages and labour standards for crew.

Agriculture
1. Learning from Self Help Groups
Source: Business Standard
Context An opinion piece arguing that the Self Help Group movement is the most significant innovation
in the rural financial landscape in many decades, and that policymakers have misread its lessons. The
core policy question posed: whether to nurture a system coming from the grassroots and allow it to
grow naturally, or infuse growth hormones.
Self Help Groups (SHGs)
Self Help Groups are small, voluntary, community-based groups, usually comprising 10–20 members,
who come together for savings, credit, livelihood activities and mutual support.
Key Features
Members make regular savings and create a common fund.
The group provides small loans to members, reducing dependence on informal moneylenders.
SHGs promote financial inclusion, entrepreneurship and livelihood generation.
They are an important tool for women’s economic empowerment in rural areas.
SHGs are often linked with banks for access to formal credit.
What Made SHGs Work
Savings first, not credit. The movement started with savings — a counter-intuitive initiative in a rural
financial system where credit had always been the credo. This inversion is the movement's
foundational insight.
Women at the centre. It targeted poor rural women, circumventing the patriarchal assumption that
the male head of household should be at the centre of poverty intervention. The author argues this
was not merely a gender argument but fundamentally a product-design argument.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 69 of 200
“The day you do not feel like studying is the day it counts most.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
How the SHG Model Works
Members save small, fixed amounts regularly, usually weekly or monthly.
Savings are collected during local group meetings.
The accumulated fund is used to provide loans to members.
Loans are provided based on mutual trust and group-based decision-making.
SHGs effectively function as local financial institutions.
Later, bank linkage enabled SHGs to access larger institutional loans.
Key Lesson — Savings First
The article challenges the idea that poor households cannot save.
Poor households generally value:
Safety
Proximity
Easy transactions
Regular savings
Trust
The success of SHGs shows that small and regular savings can become an important source of
financial security.
Problem with the Current Approach
Excessive focus on subsidised credit, grants and external funding can weaken the original savings-led
model.
SHGs may accumulate funds but have limited productive lending.
Defaults can restrict further lending.
Excessive dependence on external credit can reduce the importance of members' own savings.
Innovative Example — Insurance + Savings
An SHG in Ananthapur, Andhra Pradesh, developed an approach to make insurance more accessible:
Members opted for an annual insurance policy.
The group provided an interest-free loan to pay the annual premium.
The premium was effectively converted into a monthly savings-like arrangement.
Group profits were also used to support members.
2. Reorienting PM-KISAN
Source: Business Standard
Context The Union Cabinet's decision to extend PM-KISAN for five years from 2026-27 to 2030-31 is an
expected continuation of a programme providing annual income support to farmers. The editorial
argues the extension is an opportunity to reorient the scheme — specifically, to extend it to tenant and
landless farmers and to use its architecture to reform the fertiliser subsidy.
PM-KISAN — Current Position
Provides ₹6,000 annually to eligible farmers in three instalments.
Uses Aadhaar authentication and digital verification.
Around 95 million farmers have benefited.
The scheme has been extended for five years from 2026-27 to 2030-31.
The annual support has remained at ₹6,000 since the scheme began.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 70 of 200
“The day you do not feel like studying is the day it counts most.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Key Issue — Tenant Farmers
PM-KISAN primarily covers land-owning farmers, with eligibility linked to land records.
However:
India has a large number of tenant farmers and landless cultivators.
Tenant farmers are vulnerable to rising input costs and climate-related risks.
They often have limited access to institutional credit and government support.
Excluding them creates a gap between land ownership and actual cultivation.
Suggested solution: State-level verification can be used to identify genuine cultivators and extend
support to eligible tenant farmers.
Linking Fertiliser Subsidy with PM-KISAN
The editorial proposes using PM-KISAN’s existing JAM and DBT infrastructure for fertiliser subsidy delivery.
Under the proposed approach:
Fertiliser requirements could be estimated based on landholding and crop patterns.
Subsidy could be transferred directly to farmers.
This could reduce dependence on the current system of routing subsidies through fertiliser producers.
Better targeting could reduce leakages and misuse.
It could also address the excessive use of subsidised urea.

September 2026.
Institutions: the New Development Bank (Shanghai, President Dilma Rousseff) and the Contingent
Reserve Arrangement.

learning, internationalisation of higher education, FLN under NIPUN Bharat, and digital transformation.
2. National Co-operative Development Corporation (Amendment) Bill, 2026
Source: TH
Context The Minister of State for Cooperation introduced the National Co-operative Development
Corporation (Amendment) Bill, 2026 in the Lok Sabha.
About the NCDC
A statutory apex financial and developmental organisation functioning under the Ministry of
Cooperation.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 74 of 200
“Sleep is study too. A rested mind remembers.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Aspect Detail
Established 1963, under the NCDC Act, 1962
Ministry Ministry of Cooperation (created 2021; earlier under Agriculture)
Headquarters New Delhi
Aim: to plan, promote and finance nationwide programmes for the production, processing, marketing,
storage, export and import of agricultural produce, foodstuffs and notified commodities through
cooperative frameworks.
Key functions
Financial assistance and credit flow — direct loans, grants and working capital to eligible cooperative
societies and developmental institutions.
Infrastructure development — processing units, cold chains, storage godowns and marketing
infrastructure across rural networks.
Inclusive cooperative support — targeted assistance to weaker section cooperatives, including
women, SC/ST and smallholder farmer collectives.
Market integration and trade facilitation — agricultural export-import, value addition and supply
chain linkages.
Key Features of the Amendment Bill
Broadened statutory mandate — changes NCDC's core mandate from planning and promoting
programmes "through co-operative societies" to "for co-operative development", expanding its
operational scope.
Direct funding via intermediary entities — allows NCDC to extend loans and grants directly to
cooperative societies or non-cooperative entities such as specialised agencies and statutory bodies
engaged in supporting cooperative development.
Share capital participation — empowers NCDC, subject to Central Government approval, to
participate directly in the share capital of eligible cooperative societies and supporting development
entities.
Expanded scope — widens the definition of foodstuffs to cover processed and notified food items, and
removes geographical restrictions on industrial goods financing.
Enhanced operational and credit powers — new Sections 9A and 13A granting incidental powers for
smoother operations and enabling credit information sharing with the Central Government, RBI and
banks.
3. The Moon Base Programme
Context During the 9th meeting of the India–US Civil Space Joint Working Group (CSJWG) held at ISRO
headquarters in Bengaluru, NASA officially invited the Indian Space Research Organisation to join its
Moon Base programme. The invitation builds upon the growing strategic space partnership between the
two nations under the Artemis Accords.
What It Is
A NASA-led initiative to build humanity's first permanent lunar outpost near the lunar South Pole — a
sustainable surface habitat where astronauts will live, work, conduct scientific research and test deep-
space survival technologies.
Aspect Detail
Lead agency NASA
Announced 2026

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 75 of 200
“Sleep is study too. A rested mind remembers.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Aim: to establish an enduring human presence on the Moon, explore volatile water-ice resources at the
lunar South Pole, and create a technological stepping stone for future crewed missions to Mars.
Key Features
Strategic South Pole positioning — capitalises on the region's unique geology, where elevated ridges
receive near-continuous sunlight for solar energy while nearby craters shield vast deposits of water
ice in permanent shadow.
Commercial and private sector integration — heavily leverages partners such as Astrobotic, Blue
Origin, Firefly Aerospace and Intuitive Machines for cargo landers, surface rovers and autonomous
drones.
In-Situ Resource Utilisation (ISRU) — harvesting lunar regolith and extracting water ice to generate
oxygen, drinking water and rocket propellant on site.
Phased modular infrastructure — built step by step using expandable surface habitats, uncrewed
logistics landers, surface power grids and pressurised rovers.
Open scientific data architecture — designed under the Artemis Accords framework to enable
transparent international sharing of lunar scientific data and environmental observations.

Banking and Finance
1. Polymer Banknotes for ₹10 and ₹20
Source: TH
Context The government has approved the introduction of one billion polymer banknotes each of ₹10
and ₹20 for field trials, Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman informed the Rajya Sabha in a written reply.
What Was Approved
The RBI, on the recommendation of its Central Board, sent a proposal to the government under
Section 25 of the RBI Act, 1934.
The proposal covers one billion pieces each of ₹10 and ₹20 polymer banknotes for field trials, and
regular issuance in these two denominations after successful completion of field trials.
Per the RBI, these polymer banknotes are proposed to be issued alongside paper substrate-based
banknotes — not as replacements.
The Legal Basis — Section 25
Section 25 of the RBI Act, 1934 provides that the design, form and material of bank notes shall be
approved by the Central Government after consideration of the recommendations of the Central
Board of the RBI.
This is the constitutional division that matters: the RBI has the sole right to issue banknotes in India
(Section 22), but the design and material require government approval. The denomination of notes is
likewise governed by statute — Section 24 permits notes of ₹2, ₹5, ₹10, ₹20, ₹50, ₹100, ₹200, ₹500,
₹2,000, and other denominations up to ₹10,000 as the Central Government may specify.
Why Polymer Notes
Advantage Detail
Durability Polymer notes last substantially longer than cotton-based paper notes — typically two to five times
— which is why the trial targets the lowest denominations, where soiling and wear are worst
Cleanliness Resistant to moisture, dirt and grease; harder for contaminants to adhere
Security Permits features difficult to replicate — transparent windows, optically variable devices, holographic
elements
Cost over life Higher unit printing cost, offset by longer circulation life and reduced replacement volumes

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 77 of 200
“Sleep is study too. A rested mind remembers.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Advantage Detail
Environment Recyclable at end of life, though production is petroleum-based
Exam Relevance
RBI Grade B — High relevance. Currency management is a core RBI function and this is precisely the kind
of institutional detail Phase II Paper III rewards.
2. Fitch Affirms India's Sovereign Rating at BBB-
Source: TH
Context Fitch Ratings affirmed India's sovereign credit rating at BBB-, the lowest investment grade, and
retained its stable outlook, citing robust growth prospects and sound external finance fundamentals —
while flagging elevated government debt and lagging structural metrics as rating constraints.
What Fitch Said
Strengths cited
A robust growth outlook and solid external finance fundamentals.
A strengthening record of delivering macroeconomic stability and improving policy credibility, which
should underpin continued robust growth and enhance resilience — despite near-term headwinds
from the energy shock.
Constraints cited
Still-weak fiscal metrics — high deficits, debt and debt service compared with peers, despite recent
consolidation.
Lagging structural metrics, including governance indicators and GDP per capita.
Recent protests stemming from the NEET paper leaks may point to rising concerns among youth over
employment opportunities, risking fiscal spending pressures over time.

Context The Ministry of Textiles is organising 'Srijan: Voices from the Loom' in New Delhi to celebrate the
contribution of women weavers to India's handloom heritage.
What It Is
A special programme recognising the stories, skills and contributions of women weavers engaged in
India's handloom sector, giving women artisans a platform to narrate their experiences of resilience,
creativity and preservation of traditional weaving practices.
Organised by: Ministry of Textiles, Government of India.
Aim: to recognise and amplify the voices of women weavers, celebrate their role in preserving India's
weaving traditions, and highlight their contribution to the country's handloom heritage.
Key Features
Women weaver participation — brings together over 100 women weavers from six handloom
clusters.
First-person narratives — a platform for weavers to share personal journeys, challenges and
achievements.
Celebrating resilience — highlights how women artisans sustain livelihoods while overcoming social
and economic challenges.
Preserving weaving traditions — recognises women's role in transmitting traditional handloom skills
and knowledge across generations.
Anthology release — an anthology documenting the stories, journeys and voices of participating
women weavers.
Why Women Weavers
The handloom sector is the largest cottage industry in India and the second largest source of rural
employment after agriculture. Its defining demographic fact is that roughly 70% of handloom workers
are women — the highest female participation of any Indian industry.
Background: The Handloom Sector
Aspect Detail
National Handloom Day 7 August, marking the launch of the Swadeshi Movement in 1905
Employment Around 35 lakh handloom workers, roughly 70% women
Nodal Ministry Ministry of Textiles (Office of the Development Commissioner for Handlooms)
Key schemes
National Handloom Development Programme (NHDP) — cluster development, marketing, credit and
skill support.
Raw Material Supply Scheme — subsidised yarn.
Handloom Weavers' Comprehensive Welfare Scheme — insurance cover.
India Handloom Brand and the Handloom Mark — quality and authenticity certification.
Handloom Reservation Act, 1985 — reserves specified articles for exclusive production on handlooms.

2. The Indonesian Babirusa
Context Chester Zoo in the UK announced the birth of Rana, a rare babirusa piglet, amid declining wild
populations threatened by African swine fever, habitat loss and hunting in Indonesia.
What It Is
The babirusa (genus Babyrousa) — commonly called the "pig-deer" — is one of the oldest living and
strangest wild pig species. The name comes from Malay, describing its distinctive look and stag-like
upper tusks (babi = pig, rusa = deer).
Aspect Detail
Geographic range Endemic exclusively to four Indonesian islands — Sulawesi, Togian, Sula and Buru
Ecosystem Tropical rainforests, swamps and wetlands, often near riverbanks and ponds rich in aquatic
plants
IUCN status Vulnerable
3. Kerala Renamed Keralam
Context Parliament passed the Kerala (Alteration of Name) Bill, 2026, officially renaming Kerala as
Keralam, following a unanimous resolution by the State Legislative Assembly.
What It Is
A Parliamentary law amending the First Schedule of the Constitution to change the official name from
Kerala to Keralam — aligning the English legal name with its native Malayalam pronunciation and
cultural heritage.
Constitutional Provisions
Provision Content
Article 3 Empowers Parliament by law to form new States, alter State boundaries, or change the name of
any existing State
First Schedule Modified at Entry 15, which lists the names and territorial extents of States
Article 4 Provides that such a law is not deemed a constitutional amendment under Article 368, and is
passed by simple majority
Procedure to Change a State's Name
Initiation and State resolution — typically begins with the State Legislative Assembly passing a1.
resolution requesting the Centre to alter the name.
Presidential recommendation — a Bill may be introduced in either House only on the prior2.
recommendation of the President.
Reference to the State Legislature — before recommending the Bill, the President refers it to the3.
affected State Assembly to express its views within a specified period.
Non-binding State views — consultation is mandatory, but the views expressed are not binding on4.
the President or Parliament.
Introduction in Parliament — once the referral period expires or views are received.5.
Passage by simple majority — under Article 4, treated as an ordinary Bill requiring a simple majority6.
in both Houses.
Presidential assent and Gazette notification — the Act then modifies the First Schedule.7.
The key constitutional point: although the First Schedule is part of the Constitution, altering it under

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 85 of 200
“Preparation is quiet. Results are loud.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Article 3 is expressly excluded from Article 368 by Article 4. Parliament can therefore redraw the map of
India by simple majority — a striking feature of India's "indestructible Union of destructible States."
Precedents: Orissa → Odisha (2011), Uttaranchal → Uttarakhand, Pondicherry → Puducherry, United
Provinces → Uttar Pradesh, Madras → Tamil Nadu, Mysore → Karnataka.
Practice MCQs
Q1. With reference to the alteration of a State's name, consider the following statements:
a. Article 3 empowers Parliament to change the name of an existing State. b. Such a Bill may be
introduced only on the prior recommendation of the President. c. The President must refer the Bill to the
affected State Legislature for its views. d. The views expressed by the State Legislature are binding on
Parliament.
How many of the above statements are correct? (a) Only one (b) Only two (c) Only three (d) All four (e)
None
Q2. Consider the following statements:
a. A law altering the name of a State amends the First Schedule of the Constitution. b. Under Article 4,
such a law is not deemed to be a constitutional amendment for the purposes of Article 368. c. It is passed
by a simple majority in both Houses of Parliament. d. Odisha was renamed from Orissa through a similar
process in 2011.
How many of the above statements are correct? (a) Only one (b) Only two (c) Only three (d) All four (e)
None
Answer Key
(c) — Statements a, b, c are correct; statement d is wrong because the State Legislature's views are1.
consultative and not binding. Parliament may proceed even if the State disagrees — the basis for
describing India as an indestructible Union of destructible States.
(d) — All four are correct. The Article 4 exclusion from Article 368 is the most examinable constitutional2.
point.

Banking and Finance
1. UPI at 10: India's Digital Payments Revolution
Source: Indian Express
Context As the Unified Payments Interface completed 10 years of operation, Parliament passed the
Taxation and Other Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2026, which amends Section 10A of the Payment and
Settlement Systems Act, 2007, removing the statutory prohibition against levying a Merchant Discount
Rate on UPI and RuPay debit card transactions.
What UPI Is
Launched as a pilot in April 2016 by the RBI and NPCI, fully operational from August 2016.
India's real-time, interoperable mobile payment system, built on the Immediate Payment Service
(IMPS) protocol.
Allows instant bank-to-bank transfers via Virtual Payment Addresses (VPAs) or QR codes.
Over a decade it has moved from a domestic retail experiment to the backbone of India's digital
economy, handling the vast majority of non-cash consumer payments.
Evolution and Growth Drivers
Early policy vision (2012–13): the RBI's payment vision documents identified IMPS as a foundation for
rapid proliferation of mobile merchant and peer-to-person transfers.
Demonetisation and pandemic (2016 and 2020): both catalysed a structural shift away from cash
and cash-on-delivery toward contactless QR payments.
Private venture capital inflow (2019–2021): large investments into fintech firms such as Paytm and
PhonePe expanded Point-of-Sale infrastructure and user acquisition.
Zero-MDR mandate (2020): following the Nandan Nilekani Committee recommendations on
deepening digital payments, the government mandated 0% MDR on UPI and RuPay cards, subsidising
low-value transactions up to ₹2,000.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 87 of 200
“Preparation is quiet. Results are loud.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
The Proposed MDR Reform
A calibrated, threshold-based MDR is being evaluated, designed to ensure sustainability without
discouraging small merchants or individual citizens:
Category Treatment
Consumers and P2P transfers 100% free — guaranteed zero fee
Small merchants (annual turnover below ₹1–1.5
crore), including kirana stores
Zero merchant fees continue
Large commercial enterprises Nominal MDR of about 0.05% to 0.3–0.6%, applying only to
P2M transactions exceeding ₹2,000
Agriculture
1. Regenerative Farming and Climate Resilience — The Beforest Model
Source: businessline
Context With rainfall becoming increasingly unpredictable and temperatures rising, regenerative
agriculture is emerging as a way to strengthen soil, water security, biodiversity and farm resilience. The
experience of Beforest, a regenerative land restoration initiative, illustrates the approach.
What It Is
Beforest, founded in 2018, works at the intersection of agriculture, ecology and climate resilience. It
has built six collectives across Kodagu, Hyderabad, Mumbai and Bhopal, spanning about 1,300 acres
with 196 members.
What a collective is — per founder and CEO Sunith Reddy: a group of people bound by a shared
commitment to regenerative living who co-own and co-manage a single landscape, with a
minimum land requirement of about 100 acres.
The Model at Scale
Asked what would follow if every district adopted the model, Reddy described a sequence that becomes
a cycle:
Water security is established — the foundation on which everything else stands.1.
Succession progresses — bare soil disappears, green cover thickens, biodiversity climbs.2.
New diversity increases seed dispersal, which accelerates green cover, which attracts more diversity.3.
Regenerative Agriculture
A set of practices aimed at rebuilding soil health, water cycles and biodiversity rather than merely
sustaining current yields. Core practices: minimal tillage, permanent soil cover, crop and species
diversity, integration of trees and livestock, and elimination of synthetic inputs.
Distinguish from adjacent concepts:
Organic farming — defined by the absence of synthetic inputs; certification-based.
Natural farming (ZBNF/Bharatiya Prakritik Krishi Paddhati) — low-cost, input-free, using on-farm
preparations such as jeevamrit.
Agroforestry — integration of trees with crops or livestock.
Regenerative agriculture — outcome-defined: is soil organic carbon, water retention and biodiversity
improving?
Exam Relevance
NABARD Grade A — Very high relevance for Phase II Paper II. This touches several core areas at once:

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 88 of 200
“Preparation is quiet. Results are loud.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
watershed development (NABARD's flagship, through the Watershed Development Fund), agroforestry,
climate-resilient agriculture, and financing gaps in green agriculture. The observation that
institutional funding is almost absent from regenerative land restoration is directly a NABARD question
— it is precisely the kind of long-gestation, ecology-first investment that commercial lenders avoid and a
development bank exists to fund. Connect to NABARD's role with the Green Climate Fund, Adaptation
Fund and NAFCC, and to FPO-based aggregation as the smallholder route to the same practices.
2. Precision Agriculture — ICAR's Network Programme
Source: PIB
Context ICAR informed Parliament of its programme of national importance, the Network Programme on
Precision Agriculture (ICAR-NePPA), involving 16 ICAR Research Institutes across different domains.
What It Is
Precision agriculture applies inputs — water, nutrients, pesticides — in the right quantity, at the right
place, at the right time, instead of uniformly across a field. The gain comes from matching input to
actual need: yield rises and waste falls at the same time.
ICAR-NePPA develops technologies using sensors, remote sensing (ground, drone, satellite), AI and
ICTs, across five domains:
Domain Focus
Crop and soil Spatio-temporal monitoring and management of crop and soil health
Post-harvest Sensor-based quality monitoring — mango, banana, pulses, rice
Vertical farming IoT-based precision farming for high-value vegetables under controlled environment
Aquaculture Sensor-based monitoring of the aquatic environment
Livestock IoT-enabled health monitoring
Technologies Developed
Water and nutrients: sensor-based irrigation, drip fertigation, precision subsurface drip, poly-
mulching, raised-bed cultivation, modified raised and sunken conservation beds.
Machinery and drones: drone-based spraying, AI-enabled precision planters, variable rate fertiliser
applicator.
Controlled environment: low-cost protected cultivation, hydroponics, aeroponics, agrivoltaic
farming, agro-aqua land configuration.
Sensing and quality instruments: SPAD meter 2.0 (leaf chlorophyll), rapid Aflatoxin-B1 detection in
maize, IoT cold-room control, AI grain analyser, AI rice-seed quality assessment, image-processing
contamination measurement in seed cotton, computer-vision ginning percentage measurement,
digital twin-based fruit quality monitoring, and blockchain-enabled banana supply chain
traceability.
Digital tools: the Dweep Microclimate Monitor; a Remote Pilot Training Organisation (RPTO) at
Dehradun for UAV training.
Aquaculture: a tank-based super-intensive precision shrimp farming system yielding 120–150
tonnes/ha/year over three cycles, with smart automation and strict biosecurity.

NABARD  CURRENT  AFFAIRS
1. Agriculture Current Affairs in News
1. IIHT SUTRA 2026 Conclave on Handloom Technology
Union Minister Giriraj Singh inaugurated IIHT SUTRA 2026, the first national conclave of Indian Institutes of
Handloom Technology. Held under the theme "Weaving the Future of the Indian Handloom Sector –
Educate. Innovate", it focused on education, research, skills and innovation. MoUs were signed with
Grasim Industries, NITRA and IIM Sambalpur.
2. 'Srijan: Voices from the Loom' Celebrates Women Weavers
The Ministry of Textiles organised 'Srijan: Voices from the Loom' to recognise the contribution of women
weavers to India's handloom heritage. The event highlighted their personal journeys, achievements and
role in sustaining traditional weaving. An anthology featuring the stories and experiences of women
weavers was also released.
3. PCIM&H and IPC Renew MoU for 'One Herb, One Standard'
The Pharmacopoeia Commission for Indian Medicine & Homoeopathy (PCIM&H) and Indian
Pharmacopoeia Commission (IPC) renewed their MoU for the 'One Herb, One Standard' initiative. The
programme aims to establish uniform quality standards for medicinal plants used across different Indian
systems of medicine. The renewed agreement will also support international quality alignment and
exports.
4. Three New Cascade Frog Species Discovered in Northeast India
Scientists led by Prof. S. D. Biju of the University of Delhi discovered three new cascade frog species in
Northeast India. They are Ammachi's Cascade Frog, Sendenyu Cascade Frog and Beautiful Cascade
Frog. The discovery was based on extensive fieldwork, DNA sequencing and morphological studies,
highlighting India's rich amphibian biodiversity.

2. Small Modular Reactors (SMRs)
Source: Business Standard
Context Under the newly launched Nuclear Energy Mission, the Government informed the Lok Sabha
that it plans to develop and operationalise at least five indigenous Small Modular Reactors by 2033. This
supports India's goal of 100 GWe of nuclear capacity by 2047.
What is a Small Modular Reactor?
An advanced nuclear reactor with lower electrical output than conventional gigawatt-scale plants. As
defined by the IAEA, an SMR typically produces up to 300 MWe per unit.
Aspect Detail
India's nodal developer Bhabha Atomic Research Centre (BARC), under the Department of Atomic Energy
Legal framework SHANTI Act, 2025 — Sustainable Harnessing and Advancement of Nuclear Energy for
Transforming India Act
The SHANTI Act, 2025 enables private sector participation in nuclear technology manufacturing and
deployment — the change that makes SMR scale-up possible.
Aim: deliver clean, flexible, low-carbon baseload electricity and industrial heat, while cutting initial
capital cost, construction time and land requirement compared with large nuclear plants.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 95 of 200
“Twenty facts a day is six hundred a month. Start with today's.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
How SMRs Work
Nuclear fission — heavy nuclei such as Uranium-235 are split in a controlled chain reaction to1.
produce heat
Modular factory fabrication — major components are built in centralised factories and shipped to2.
site for rapid assembly, instead of a decade of site-specific civil construction
Heat conversion — the heat produces steam to drive turbines, or is used directly for high-3.
temperature industrial processes
3. Reports / Articles in News
1. NITI Aayog Report on Key Sectors for Global Manufacturing Hub
Source: PIB
Context NITI Aayog has released a report titled "Key Sectors to Position India as a Global Manufacturing
Hub." It uses a structured, data-driven framework to identify sectors that can drive India's ambition to
become a global manufacturing powerhouse. This edition covers four sectors — Chemicals, Textiles,
Telecom and Networking Equipment, and Solar Photovoltaic manufacturing — with reports on eight
more sectors to follow.
What the Report Does
The assessment looks at India's manufacturing position against global trends, sectoral growth
opportunities and global benchmarks. It examines seven factors that decide whether a country can
compete in manufacturing.
The aim: find sectors where India has real growth potential and where targeted action can strengthen
domestic capability, raise competitiveness and value addition, and accelerate export-oriented
manufacturing.
Key Highlights:
Sector 1: Chemicals
Main areas: Petrochemicals, specialty chemicals and inorganic chemicals.
India should increase downstream production of higher-value products.
Focus on domestic manufacturing, better use of raw materials and strategic FTAs to reduce imports.
Sector 2: Textiles
Contributes about 2% of GDP, 11% of manufacturing GVA and 9% of merchandise exports.
Employs over 45 million people and exports about $37.7 billion annually.
Focus on MMF, technical and sustainable textiles, along with better raw materials, infrastructure, skills
and technology.
India needs to reduce its dependence on cotton and match the growing global demand for man-
made fibres.
Sector 3: Telecom Equipment
India is the world's second-largest telecom market, with over 1.2 billion subscribers.
Main issue: India is a large market but depends heavily on imported components.
Focus on local component production, technology transfer, industrial clusters, R&D and exports.
Sector 4: Solar PV
Solar capacity stood at 106 GW in March 2025, with a target of 280 GW by 2030.
India has strong module production but still imports many cells and wafers.
Build the full chain: polysilicon → wafers → cells → modules through technology, R&D, investment and

skilled workers.
Common Theme
India needs to move beyond basic production and assembly and capture more value within the
country. The focus is on domestic value addition, lower import dependence and stronger global
competitiveness.
Practice MCQs
Q1. With reference to the NITI Aayog report on key manufacturing sectors, consider the following
statements:
a. It covers chemicals, textiles, telecom and networking equipment, and solar photovoltaic
manufacturing.
b. The study was structured into four phases, beginning with shortlisting sectors by relative
attractiveness.
c. Reports on eight more sectors are to follow.
d. The report assesses only market size and ignores infrastructure and technology readiness.
How many of the above statements are correct? (a) Only one (b) Only two (c) Only three (d) All four (e)
None
Q2. With reference to India's textile and apparel sector as described in the report, consider the following
statements:
a. It contributes about 11 per cent to manufacturing gross value added.
b. It accounts for about 9 per cent of merchandise exports.
c. It employs more than 45 million people and is the second-largest employer after agriculture.
d. India is the second-largest textile exporter in the world.
How many of the above statements are correct? (a) Only one (b) Only two (c) Only three (d) All four (e)
None
Answer Key
(c) — Statements a, b, c are correct. Statement d is wrong: the report assesses seven factors including1.
infrastructure readiness, technology readiness, raw material availability and India's position in the
value chain, not market size alone.
(c) — Statements a, b, c are correct. Statement d is wrong: India is the sixth-largest textile exporter2.
globally, with a 4.1% share of global textile and apparel exports.

1. Economy in News

India and the Southern African Customs Union (SACU) signed Terms of Reference for negotiations

Former Chinese Premier Zhu Rongji passed away at the age of 97. He served as China's Premier from
1998 to 2003 and played an important role in economic reforms, including restructuring state-owned
enterprises and strengthening fiscal and financial systems. He also played a key role in China's WTO
accession in 2001.
2. Social Issue in News
1. Joint Parliamentary Committee (JPC)
Source: Indian Express
Context The Lok Sabha referred the Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Amendment Bill, 2026 to a Joint
Parliamentary Committee, following strong objections from Opposition parties and civil society.
What is a JPC?
An ad hoc (temporary) parliamentary committee set up for detailed, clause-by-clause examination
of a controversial Bill, a complex legislative proposal, or a major financial or governance issue.
Aspect Detail
Constitutional status Not mentioned in the Constitution
Governing rules Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business of both Houses
How formed A motion moved in one House and concurred with by the other
Chairperson Appointed by the Speaker of the Lok Sabha
Composition ratio Lok Sabha members typically twice the number from the Rajya Sabha (2:1)

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 98 of 200
“Twenty facts a day is six hundred a month. Start with today's.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Aspect Detail
Party strength Membership and chairmanship reflect relative party strengths, giving the ruling side a
majority
After the report The committee automatically stands dissolved
Key Functions
Clause-by-clause scrutiny to identify administrative, legal or constitutional loopholes
Summoning power — may call for official records and papers, and summon officials, experts and
stakeholders
Public consultation — invites written submissions and holds oral hearings with affected organisations,
industry bodies and civil society
Recommendations and dissent — prepares an advisory report; dissenting members may append
formal Notes of Dissent
Practice MCQs
Q1. With reference to a Joint Parliamentary Committee, consider the following statements:
a. It is not explicitly mentioned in the Constitution of India.
b. It is constituted when a motion moved in one House is adopted and concurred with by the other.
c. Its chairperson is appointed by the Speaker of the Lok Sabha.
d. It is a permanent committee that continues across Parliaments.
How many are correct? (a) One (b) Two (c) Three (d) All four (e) None
Answer Key
(c) — a, b, c correct. Statement d is wrong: a JPC is an ad hoc committee, dissolved automatically1.
once it submits its report — unlike standing committees, which are reconstituted annually.
3. Misc GA in News
1. Sumit Sarkar, Eminent Historian, Passes Away at 87
Eminent Marxist historian Sumit Sarkar, a noted scholar of modern Indian history, passed away at the
age of 87 in New Delhi. He taught history at the University of Delhi and was a Fellow of Wolfson College,
Oxford. He was also a visiting professor at several universities in the US and Europe.
2. India–Malaysia Exercise Udara Shakti 2026
The Indian Air Force (IAF) and Royal Malaysian Air Force (RMAF) commenced the third edition of
bilateral Exercise Udara Shakti 2026 in Malaysia. The exercise focuses on improving interoperability,
operational coordination and defence cooperation. India deployed Rafale fighters and C-17 aircraft,
while Malaysia participated with Su-30MKM, F/A-18D and A400M aircraft.
3. International Left-Handers Day 2026 – August 13
International Left-Handers Day is observed annually on 13 August to celebrate left-handed people and
highlight the challenges they face. The day was conceptualised by Dean Robert Campbell in 1976 and
officially launched by the Left-Handers Club in 1992. It promotes greater awareness of left-handedness in
a predominantly right-handed world.

1. Black-necked Crane
Context The Bombay Natural History Society (BNHS), working with the Indian Army and local
authorities, has sterilised over 2,950 free-ranging dogs in Ladakh since 2022 to reduce predation on the
Black-necked Crane and its chicks.
What is the Black-necked Crane?
The only exclusively alpine species among the world's 15 crane species.
Aspect Detail
Local names Thrung Thrung Karmo (Dzongkha), Cha Tung-Tung (Ladakhi)
State Bird of Union Territory of Ladakh
IUCN Red List Near Threatened (NT)
Range countries India, China, Bhutan, Nepal
2. Fertiliser Subsidy Rises Despite Low Nutrient-Use Efficiency
India is spending more on fertiliser subsidies, while only around 40% of applied nutrients are absorbed
by crops.
Unused nutrients can cause:
Groundwater contamination
Air pollution
Nutrient wastage
Higher subsidy burden
The issue highlights the need to improve Nutrient-Use Efficiency (NUE).

Saptadhara means seven streams. It refers to the seven strategic areas of India's growth and

2. RBI Closes FCNR(B) Swap Window
Source: IE
Context The Reserve Bank of India has decided to close its swap facility for FCNR(B) deposits one month
ahead of schedule, after banks mobilised $52.3 billion under the scheme by 13 August. The decision
comes barely a week after Governor Sanjay Malhotra said on 5 August that there was no proposal to
withdraw the scheme.
What is an FCNR(B) Deposit?
FCNR(B) stands for Foreign Currency Non-Resident (Bank) deposit.
It is a term deposit held in foreign currency by a non-resident Indian with an Indian bank. Both the
principal and interest are paid in the same foreign currency, so the depositor faces no rupee
conversion risk.
What is the Swap Window?
Launched on 8 June 2026, the facility lets banks swap the foreign currency they raise through FCNR(B)
deposits with the RBI.
How it works:
The bank collects dollars from NRI depositors
It sells those dollars to the RBI and receives rupees
At maturity, the transaction reverses at the same exchange rate
RBI bears the full hedging cost. Because the exchange rate is fixed at both ends, the bank carries no
currency risk.
That is what allows banks to offer attractive interest rates to NRI depositors.
3. L&T to Build NVIDIA B300 AI Factory in Chennai
Larsen & Toubro (L&T) secured a ₹10,000–15,000 crore order to build a large AI computing facility for
Together AI.
The facility will be located at L&T's Chennai data-centre campus.
It will deploy 10,000 NVIDIA B300 GPUs.
The facility will support AI training, inference and model fine-tuning.
4. NCLT Launches e-Inspection and e-Certified Copy Services
The National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT) launched e-Inspection and e-Certified Copy Services.
Users can digitally inspect case records and obtain certified copies through the NCLT e-Filing portal.
The initiative is part of the e-Courts 2.0 programme.
It aims to reduce physical visits and improve the speed, transparency and accessibility of judicial
records.
NCLT was established on 1 June 2016 under the Companies Act, 2013.

1. Reports in News
1. US Transshipment Report
Source: IE
Context The White House Office of Trade and Manufacturing Policy has named India among more than
40 countries and trading jurisdictions exposed to Chinese-linked transshipment risks, in a report titled
'The Great Transshipment Scam'. India has been placed in Tier-1 of what the report calls China's
Shadow Transshipment Network.
What is Illegal Transshipment?
The report defines it as routing goods through a third country where they undergo:
Minor processing
Relabelling
Repackaging
Re-invoicing
Changes in paperwork
The Three Tiers
Tier Countries Basis of classification
Tier 1 India, Mexico, Canada, European Union Illegal transshipment risk is "embedded" within massive
legitimate trade flows
Tier 2 Vietnam, Malaysia, Thailand "Closely integrated" with China
Tier 3 Cambodia, Panama, UAE "Weak customs enforcement"
Read Tier 1 carefully. India is not placed there for weak enforcement or close integration with China. It is
placed there because its trade with the US is so large that suspect flows can hide inside legitimate ones.
That is a very different accusation — and a much harder one to disprove.
Corridors named in the report:
Corridor Product US region affected
Pune–Gujarat–Chennai (India) Pumps and compressors Cincinnati, Dayton, Columbus
Ho Chi Minh City (Vietnam) Electrical switching and circuit
protection
Chicago, Milwaukee, Rockford
Penang–Kulim (Malaysia) Plastic articles Akron, Canton, Upstate South Carolina
Bekasi–Batam (Indonesia) Plastic boxes, cases, crates,
packing
Houston, Lake Charles, Beaumont, Tulsa
Ayutthaya–Samut Prakan (Thailand) Thermostats Minneapolis–St. Paul
