Agriculture
1. Nepal Flash Flood 2026
Source: Indian Express
Context On 26 August 2026, a devastating flash flood struck the mountainous region along the
Nepal–China border.
What Happened
Worst-affected areas: Rasuwa, Nuwakot, Dhading and Chitwan in Nepal
Also affected Tibet's Gyirong County and Gyirong Port
A major torrent of water, ice, rock, mud and debris rushed downstream through Himalayan river
valleys
Villages, bridges, roads, hydropower infrastructure and other facilities were destroyed
The flood travelled through the Bhote Koshi–Trishuli/Narayani–Gandak river system
Because this system connects with northern India, authorities in Bihar and Uttar Pradesh monitored
the situation
Nepal's government described extensive damage to homes, schools, hospitals, roads, bridges and
hydropower plants
What Triggered It?
This was not a conventional monsoon flood. The disaster developed through a rapid chain of events:
Glacier / ice-rock collapse
↓
Ice and rock avalanche
↓
Large amount of debris enters the river/tributary
↓
River gets temporarily blocked or its flow is suddenly displaced
↓
Water + ice + mud + rocks surge downstream
↓
Flash flood + powerful debris flow
↓
Destruction of settlements and infrastructure
Was It a GLOF?
A GLOF (Glacial Lake Outburst Flood) occurs when a glacial lake suddenly releases a large volume of
water, usually because its natural dam fails.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 184 of 200
“The best time to revise was yesterday. The next best is now.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
In this case: the initial trigger was a glacier / ice-rock collapse and avalanche, not a classic GLOF.
But: the disaster subsequently created or affected temporary barrier lakes, raising the possibility of
another flood if those unstable lakes breach.
Why the Himalayas Are Becoming More Vulnerable
Rising temperatures — the Hindu Kush Himalaya (HKH) region is warming rapidly. Higher temperatures:
Accelerate glacier melting
Weaken glacier structures
Destabilise mountain slopes
Increase rockfalls and avalanches
Contribute to formation and expansion of glacial lakes
Destabilise permafrost
Increase the probability of sudden flood events
2. The DAANVEER Initiative
Source: PIB
Context The Ministry of Panchayati Raj called for nationwide public participation under the 'DAANVEER'
initiative — encouraging citizens, CSR entities and the Indian diaspora to donate desktop computers to
verified Gram Panchayats.
What It Is
A technology-enabled, people-led Computer Crowdsourcing Initiative
Allows individuals, philanthropic organisations, CSR partners and the diaspora to directly purchase
and donate standardised desktop computers
To state-verified Gram Panchayats needing digital hardware
About
Nodal Ministry: Panchayati Raj (MoPR)
Complements: infrastructure funding under the Rashtriya Gram Swaraj Abhiyan (RGSA)
Aim: bridge the rural digital divide and strengthen grassroots digital governance by augmenting IT
hardware across Gram Panchayats through voluntary, transparent public contributions.
Key Features
App-to-marketplace integration: Select a verified Panchayat on Meri Panchayat App → purchase
through DigiHaat (ONDC) with pre-filled details.
Standardised hardware: Donors choose government-approved computer bundles meeting required
technical specifications.
State-level verification: Only Panchayats officially identified as lacking IT infrastructure are listed.
Real-time tracking: Track the entire process from payment and dispatch to delivery and receipt.
Digital recognition: Panchayat Secretary confirms delivery → donor receives a verifiable Certificate of
Appreciation.
3. Ammannia telanganensis
Source: New Indian Express
Context A new freshwater flowering plant species discovered in Telangana has been named
Ammannia telanganensis and described in the international taxonomy journal Phytotaxa.

August 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 185 of 200
“The best time to revise was yesterday. The next best is now.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
What It Is
An annual, herbaceous, semi-aquatic flowering plant
Belongs to the Lythraceae (loosestrife) family
A newly classified species within the genus Ammannia — a group of wetland-adapted herbs found in
tropical and subtropical freshwater habitats
Habitat and Distribution
Ecosystem: grows in wet, sandy soils along seasonal streams, temporary puddles and marshy
margins of freshwater bodies
Where found:
Rangareddy district — Narsingi and Gachibowli
Nagarkurnool district — Vatwarlapalle, in the Amrabad Tiger Reserve
Associated flora: co-exists with Ammannia multiflora and various Rotala species
Flowering and fruiting: post-monsoon through winter — November to February
Key Terms
Pedunculate — borne on a stalk; sessile — attached directly, without a stalk
Apetalous — lacking petals
Testa — the outer seed coat
Cyme — a flower cluster in which the central flower opens first
Riparian — relating to the bank of a river or stream

Context NABARD and the National Bank for Financing Infrastructure and Development (NaBFID) signed

6. Climate-Proofing India's Food System
Source: The Hindu
Context India has strong food production and buffer stocks, but must build a climate-resilient,
sustainable food-security system to protect agriculture, supply chains and the PDS from rising
temperatures, erratic monsoons and groundwater depletion.
What Climate-Proofing Means
Redesigning national agricultural, procurement, storage and public distribution policies to
withstand climate change
Shifting focus away from volumetric calorie production through monoculture
Toward "sustainable food and nutrition security"
Five Pillars of a Climate-Resilient Food System
Diversified procurement: Expand MSP beyond rice and wheat to millets, pulses and oilseeds suited to
local climates.
Diversified PDS: Add millets and pulses to PDS, PM POSHAN and Anganwadi programmes for better
nutrition and lower water use.
Climate-proof storage: Develop flood-resilient silos, temperature-controlled storage and cold
chains.
Weather-based planning: Use IMD forecasts for buffer stocks, MSP procurement and district-level
contingency planning.
Sustainable farming: Promote micro-irrigation, climate-resilient seeds and weather-indexed crop
insurance under PM Fasal Bima Yojana.

7. Global Drought Resilience Architecture: DRI and DRIF
Source: Times of India
Context At UNCCD COP17, a new global drought resilience architecture was launched to shift from
reactive crisis response to proactive preparedness — featuring the world's first Drought Resilience
Index (DRI) and Drought Resilience Investment Facility (DRIF).
Drought Resilience Index (DRI)
What it is: the world's first comprehensive technical monitoring metric to evaluate a country's
systemic capacity to handle and withstand drought.
Aim: enable countries to assess their capacity to anticipate, prepare for and adapt to droughts, while
identifying structural vulnerabilities to inform data-driven policies and investments.
Key features:
AI-powered analytics — helps policymakers, technical experts and the public interpret complex
climate and geographical data into practical insights
Vulnerability mapping — pinpoints regional and national vulnerabilities, allowing governments to
target high-risk areas before a drought escalates into a humanitarian crisis
Proactive paradigm shift — from reactive disaster relief to technology-enabled resilience and
preparedness
Policy and investment guide — turns raw metrics into actionable intelligence on where to allocate
resources
Drought Resilience Investment Facility (DRIF)
What it is: the world's first global catalytic financing platform dedicated exclusively to funding and
scaling drought resilience projects.
Aim: use public capital to reduce investment risk, thereby unlocking much larger flows of
institutional and private finance.
8. Government Introduces Fortnightly Sugar Allocation System
The Centre will introduce a fortnightly sugar allocation system from September, replacing the
monthly quota system.
Mills must sell at least 40% of their allocation in the first week.
The remaining allocation must be sold in the following week.
Mills have also been directed to dispatch sold sugar within one week, aimed at improving market
supply.

The scheme has been extended up to 31 March 2030.
It provides collateral-free working-capital loans to street vendors, with a 7% interest subsidy on
timely repayment.

2. RBI Relaxes FCNR(B) Swap Access for Large Transactions
RBI allowed banks to access its concessional dollar-rupee swap facility outside their designated

Banks receiving such inflows can now access the facility on the same day instead of waiting for their
allotted day.
The move aims to reduce the lag between mobilisation of funds and transfer to RBI.

1. Russia Remains India’s Largest Crude Oil Supplier
India’s Russian crude imports are expected at around 2.1 million barrels per day (mbpd) in August
2026.
This is lower than the record 2.6 mbpd recorded in June and July.
Despite the decline, Russia continues to account for more than 40% of India’s crude imports.
Lower imports reflect factors such as refinery maintenance, reduced Russian availability and
increased competition from China, rather than weakening Indian demand alone.

1. Trishuli River System Hit by Catastrophic Flash Flood
A catastrophic flash flood originating from a dammed lake in Tibet affected the Trishuli river system in
Nepal.
The event followed a 4.4-magnitude earthquake and sent an estimated 20 million cubic metres of
excess water downstream.
The affected system includes the Bhote Koshi–Trishuli–Narayani rivers.
The Trishuli is a transboundary, snow-fed Himalayan river and an important tributary of the
Gandaki/Narayani basin.

It would become SpaceX’s fourth US launch site and second Starship-focused launch complex.

It will serve as a versatile platform for testing indigenous naval weapons and sensors.

1. Integrated Fertilizer Management System (iFMS)
Source: Mint
Context The government is turning its fertiliser management into a data-driven platform, linking sales
with farmer, land and crop records and tracking supplies from production to retail — through the
Integrated Fertilizer Management System (iFMS).
Aim: spot supply gaps and unusual buying patterns early, while making subsidy payouts more
transparent.
What is iFMS?
The Integrated Fertilizer Management System (iFMS) is a web-based system of the Government of
India used to monitor and manage the availability, movement, distribution and sale of fertilizers
across the country.
It helps the government track fertilizers from manufacturers/importers → wholesalers → retailers →
farmers.
Key Objectives
Ensure timely availability of fertilizers to farmers.
Monitor the supply and movement of fertilizers across States and districts.
Track fertilizer stocks at different levels.
Help prevent shortages, diversion and black marketing.
Support better management of fertilizer subsidy.
Provide data for policy-making and supply planning.
How does it work?
Production/Import
↓
Fertilizer companies
↓
Movement to States
↓
Wholesalers
↓
Retailers
↓
Farmers

sales.
2. The Spoon-tailed Duskhawker
Source: TOI
Context Researchers documented the first verifiable photographic record of the rare Spoon-tailed
Duskhawker (Gynacantha basiguttata) along the Miao-Vijaynagar Road inside Namdapha National
Park and Tiger Reserve, Arunachal Pradesh.
About the species
Scientific name: Gynacantha basiguttata
Common name: Spoon-tailed Duskhawker
Order: Odonata
Family: Aeshnidae
Type: Large predatory dragonfly
It is associated with Southeast Asia, with the species originally described from the Philippines.
It is notable for its spoon-shaped terminal appendages, which give it its common name.
It is also known for crepuscular behaviour, meaning it is particularly active around dawn and dusk.
About Namdapha National Park
Located in Changlang district, Arunachal Pradesh
India's easternmost tiger reserve
The only protected area in the world where four large cats occur: tiger, leopard, snow leopard and
clouded leopard
Home to the endemic Namdapha flying squirrel (Critically Endangered)
Borders Myanmar
3. Neelakurinji-like Bloom in Kudremukh Hills
Source: The Hindu
Context A rare blue-violet flower resembling Neelakurinji has bloomed along trekking trails in the
Kudremukh region of Chikkamagaluru district, Karnataka — attracting trekkers and nature enthusiasts.
Neelakurinji-like Bloom in Kudremukh Hills
A mass flowering event has added a striking blue-purple appearance to parts of the Kudremukh Hills
in Karnataka.
The flowering has been compared to the famous Neelakurinji (Strobilanthes kunthiana) bloom of the
Western Ghats.
About Neelakurinji
Scientific name: Strobilanthes kunthiana
Family: Acanthaceae
Famous for blooming once in 12 years
Found in the shola grasslands of the Western Ghats, chiefly in Kerala, Tamil Nadu and Karnataka
The Nilgiri Hills take their name from it — nilgiri meaning "blue mountains"
Added to Schedule III of the Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972 in 2024, giving it legal protection
The last major bloom in the Munnar region was in 2018

About Kudremukh
Located in Chikkamagaluru district, Karnataka, in the Western Ghats
Kudremukh National Park is the second largest protected area of tropical wet evergreen forest in
the Western Ghats
The name means "horse face" in Kannada, from the shape of a peak
Kudremukh peak is among Karnataka's highest, at about 1,894 m
Source of three rivers: the Tunga, Bhadra and Nethravathi
Part of the Western Ghats UNESCO World Heritage Site
Notable for shola-grassland ecosystems, the habitat where Kurinji species occur
4. Carcass Dump Relocation Reduces Vulture Electrocution in Dehradun
Source: The Hindu
Context A decade-old study published in the Journal of Threatened Taxa suggests that relocating a
livestock carcass dump 2.4 km away from high-tension power infrastructure near Dehradun,
Uttarakhand, may have saved vultures from electrocution.
The Threats Vultures Face
Poisoning
Veterinary non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs)
Food scarcity
Habitat loss
Important Facts for Exams
Study published in the Journal of Threatened Taxa; conducted at Sudhauwala, Dehradun
Species affected: Himalayan griffon, Eurasian griffon, cinereous vulture
Wildlife Institute of India is based at Dehradun
Diclofenac was banned for veterinary use in 2006; meloxicam is the safe substitute
Indian vulture species: White-rumped, Indian (long-billed), Slender-billed, Red-headed, Egyptian,
Himalayan griffon, Eurasian griffon, Cinereous, Bearded
White-rumped, Indian, Slender-billed and Red-headed vultures are Critically Endangered (IUCN)
Action Plan for Vulture Conservation 2020-25; Vulture Conservation Breeding Centres at Pinjore,
Rajabhatkhawa and Rani

What It Is

Javelin is a fire-and-forget, infrared-guided anti-tank missile.
Relevant to India–US defence cooperation and defence indigenisation.

5. YJ-18A Missile
China reportedly tested a new stealth-coated YJ-18A anti-ship cruise missile variant.
Radar-absorbing coating is intended to reduce radar reflection.
Important Defence & Science and Technology current affair.
6. Meera Behn & Subhadra Kumari Chauhan
Indian Coast Guard decommissioned two Fast Patrol Vessels after around two decades of service.
Used for maritime security and coastal surveillance.
Important Defence GA.
7. China–Bangladesh 2+2 Dialogue
China and Bangladesh are preparing for their first 2+2 diplomatic and defence dialogue.
Combines foreign affairs and defence-level engagement.
Important for India’s neighbourhood and regional geopolitics.
8. Sone River Water Pact
Bihar and Jharkhand are scheduled to sign a Sone River water-sharing agreement.
Intended to resolve a 25-year-old inter-state water dispute.
Important for federalism, river-water sharing and Bihar/Jharkhand geography.
